# Canonical Utilities - Full Rationale

The complete, reasoned entry for every commonly-reimplemented function in the codebase. The always-loaded index in [CLAUDE.md](../../CLAUDE.md#commonly-reimplemented-functions-do-not-add-new-copies) lists each entry as ONE line (name, canonical symbols, path) under the same group headings used here; this file carries the why - the failure each canonical implementation replaced, the invariants it protects, and the traps that made the duplicates subtly wrong. Read the matching entry here BEFORE extending or working around one of these utilities. Find it by its title: `grep -n "Title of the entry" docs/agent-guides/CANONICAL-UTILITIES.md`.

`npm run docs:verify` checks that every path below still resolves, that every entry here has an index line, and that no index line has grown past one line of symbols and paths. Rationale belongs in this file, never in the index.

## Formatting, IDs, and pricing

- **Browser relay text chunks:** `chunkBrowserRelayText(text)` in `src/shared/browserRelay.ts` yields strings of at most 16,384 UTF-16 code units, matching the host input validator. Native streamed pages and remote browser tabs use it for paste, composition, and textarea input. Sending a whole large paste used to reject it; slicing just the prefix would silently lose the rest. The generator preserves surrogate pairs at chunk boundaries. Consume it inside one existing queued operation and await each IPC call so later keystrokes cannot interleave and a large paste cannot exhaust the queue's operation limit. Recheck disposal or lease ownership between chunks; never replay abandoned input after reconnection.
- **ID generation:** `generateId()` in `src/renderer/utils/ids.ts`, `generateUUID()` in `src/shared/uuid.ts`
- **Format file size:** `formatSize()` in `src/shared/formatters.ts`
- **Truncate prose without splitting a word:** `truncateAtWordBoundary(text, maxLength, ellipsis?)` in `src/shared/formatters.ts`. Cuts at the last space that fits and counts the ellipsis toward `maxLength`, falling back to a hard cut when there is no space. Tab naming caps generated names with it. Use `truncateText()` / `truncateCommand()` instead for single tokens or commands, where a mid-word cut is fine.
- **Format numbers:** `formatNumber()` (compact: `1.2M`) and `formatCount()` (exact, grouped: `1,204,993`) in `src/shared/formatters.ts`. Pick by whether the digits ARE the information - a badge wants the magnitude, a filtered row count wants the number.
- **Format tokens:** `formatTokens()`, `formatTokensCompact()`, `estimateTokenCount()` in `src/shared/formatters.ts`
- **Format elapsed time:** `formatElapsedTimeColon()` in `src/shared/formatters.ts`
- **Humanized durations:** everything in `src/shared/duration.ts` (re-exported from `formatters.ts`). `humanizeDuration(ms, opts)` is the one engine; `formatDurationHuman` / `Compact` / `Verbose` / `Parts` / `Decimal` / `Long` / `Words`, plus `formatActiveTime`, `formatElapsedTime`, `formatTurnDuration` (transcript turn time: day-capped, second-free, `<1m` below a minute), `formatElapsedTicker` (thinking pill: `"0m 3s"`), and `formatElapsedTickerCompact` (the same ladder without the padded lead, for a counter inside a chip: `"3s"`, `"20m 4s"`), are presets over it. **Never hand-roll another `Math.floor(ms / 86400000)` ladder** - a dozen near-identical copies had already drifted apart on where the ladder stops, whether a zero segment is padded (`"2h 0m"` vs `"2h"`), and whether a countdown rounds up. Those are options (`units`, `maxUnits`, `style`, `keepZeroUnits`, `keepLeadingZero`, `adjacentUnits`, `round`, `fallback`), so pick a preset or call the engine. Unit sizes are in `DURATION_MS` - don't redeclare `const DAY = 86400000`. See [SHARED-UTILS.md → Durations](SHARED-UTILS.md#durations-srcshareddurationts---both).
- **Format profiling spans (ms):** `formatDuration()` in `src/shared/performance-metrics.ts` (NOT formatters.ts - common mistake). Two-decimal `"123.45ms"` / `"1.23s"` for perf traces only; it caps at seconds, so a multi-day span comes out as a giant seconds float. For anything user-facing use `src/shared/duration.ts`.
- **Format relative time:** `formatRelativeTime()` in `src/shared/formatters.ts`
- **Format a calendar day:** `formatCalendarDay()` in `src/shared/formatters.ts` renders a `YYYY-MM-DD` string as `"Jul 10, 2026"`. It reads the parts out of the string and builds a LOCAL date, because `new Date('2026-07-10')` is parsed as UTC midnight and renders the day before everywhere west of Greenwich.
- **Local calendar day keys:** `toLocalYmd(msOrDate)`, `parseLocalYmd(ymd)`, `addLocalDays(ymd, n)` in `src/shared/localDay.ts`. Every per-day stat buckets by the user's LOCAL day, the boundary SQLite's `date(..., 'localtime')` draws. `toISOString()` is UTC, so it files an evening under tomorrow everywhere west of Greenwich; `new Date('2026-07-10')` is UTC midnight and lands on the day before; adding `86400000` drifts an hour across a DST change. Two private copies existed (`toLocalYmd` in `src/main/stats/utils.ts`, `parseYmd` / `toYmd` in `delegationTrendUtils.ts`); both now re-export this module for their existing callers.
- **Format an hour of day:** `formatHourOfDay(hour)` in `src/shared/formatters.ts` - `0` -> `"12 AM"`, `13` -> `"1 PM"`. The dashboard's peak-hour cards (`formatHour` in `SummaryCards.tsx` is an alias) and `maestro-cli stats` both print it. `PeakHoursChart` keeps its own lowercase `"8pm"` axis variant on purpose.
- **Format cost:** `formatCost()` in `src/shared/formatters.ts`
- **Timestamp for a generated file name:** `fileTimestampSlug(dateOrTimestamp?)` in `src/shared/formatters.ts` - `20260713-142530`, filesystem-safe everywhere and chronologically sortable. LOCAL time on purpose: the name is read by the human who pasted the image at that wall-clock moment, so a UTC stamp reads as the wrong hour to everyone outside UTC. Both the renderer's `saveImageToProject()` and the CLI's `image save` name files with it, so a screenshot saved by hand and the same one saved by an agent cannot come out under two different conventions.
- **Token pricing and Codex token totals:** `resolveModelPricing()` / `calculateModelCost()` in `src/shared/modelPricing.ts`, and `CodexTokenCounts` in `src/shared/codexTokenUsage.ts`. Pricing is not Claude-only: `resolveModelPricing` matches the OpenAI families first (`gpt-5*` / any `codex` slug, split nano / mini / frontier), since a Codex slug carries no Anthropic family word and used to fall through to the Sonnet default. The two providers also disagree about what `input_tokens` MEANS - Anthropic reports cache reads beside it, OpenAI inside it - so `PricingConfig.CACHE_READ_SUBSET_OF_INPUT` rides on the tier and `calculateWithPricing` bills only the uncached remainder; without it a cache hit costs MORE than a miss. On the parsing side, Codex's `info.total_token_usage` is a running SESSION TOTAL while `turn.completed.usage` and `info.last_token_usage` are per-turn, so summing the first makes a session report tokens proportional to the SQUARE of its turn count (1.3B real input tokens were reported as 75B). Every Codex transcript scan goes through `CodexTokenCounts`; do NOT hand-roll another sum, and remember that a derivation fix like this is invisible to the mtime/size fingerprints, so it needs `TOKEN_USAGE_CACHE_VERSION`, `GLOBAL_STATS_CACHE_VERSION` **and `CODEX_SESSION_CACHE_VERSION`** (`src/main/storage/codex-session-storage.ts`) bumped or the old numbers are served forever - that third one guards a per-session cache reused while `fileMtimeMs` still covers the file, so it is the one most easily missed.
- **Summing a Codex session's tokens:** `CodexTokenCounts` in `src/shared/codexTokenUsage.ts`. Codex reports usage in two shapes and only one is per-turn: `turn.completed.usage` and `info.last_token_usage` are that turn's numbers, while `info.total_token_usage` on a `token_count` event is the running SESSION TOTAL. Summing the total across events is the trap - a session with N turns reports 1+2+...+N times its real tokens, so cost grows with the SQUARE of the conversation length, which is what turned 1.7B input tokens into 75B and let Codex outrank Claude on a dashboard driven almost entirely by Claude agents. The accumulator prefers `last_token_usage`, falls back to a cumulative DELTA, and reads a total that moves backwards as a counter restart (a resume or fork) rather than clamping it to zero. Reasoning output folds into `outputTokens`; `cachedTokens` stays a SUBSET of `inputTokens`. All three transcript scanners go through it so they cannot disagree again - do NOT hand-roll a fourth sum. It has no Electron imports so the CLI can bundle it. **Bump `CODEX_SESSION_CACHE_VERSION` in `src/main/storage/codex-session-storage.ts` whenever this math changes**: entries are reused while `fileMtimeMs` still covers the file, and a parsing fix does not touch a transcript's mtime, so old inflated numbers are served until a session is rewritten.
- **Claude's cost and per-model usage for one turn:** `claudeTurnCost()`, `readClaudeTurnBaseline()`, `readLastClaudeCostState()`, `parseCostStateRow()` in `src/shared/claudeCostTracker.ts`. Claude has the same trap as Codex in a different place: a result's `usage` is per-turn, but its `modelUsage` and `total_cost_usd` are the SESSION's running totals, because claude restores its cost tracker on `--resume` (three turns costing 0.117, 0.098 and 0.020 reported 0.117, 0.215 and 0.235). Maestro records one row per turn, so it summed running totals and a session's recorded cost grew with the SQUARE of its turn count - in both token sources, since the TUI keeps the same tracker. The tracker is written to the transcript as a `cost-state` row each time claude exits, and the last one is exactly what the next resume restores, so this turn's spend is the reported total minus that row. The spawn reads it (`ChildProcessSpawner` primes the parser through `AgentOutputParser.primeResumedSession`; the CLI's Claude path reads it before spawning) and `ClaudeOutputParser` subtracts it before any usage event goes out. The baseline is three-valued and the values are not interchangeable: a state to subtract, `null` for a known zero (fresh session, or a transcript with no tracker), `undefined` for unknown (a remote transcript). Unknown falls back to the session's last total this app saw, then to pricing the turn's own `usage` from the rate table, never to the raw running total. A report that does not continue the baseline (any counter below it) is passed through untouched rather than subtracted. maestro-p reports running totals too, matching `claude -p`, so do NOT make it report per-turn numbers - that would make the two token sources disagree about what the field means.
- **Live tokens-per-second rate:** `createThroughputTracker()` and `THROUGHPUT_WINDOWS_MS` in `src/shared/throughputRate.ts`. The Throughput Gauge is the only consumer today. The engine is pure: the caller passes `nowMs` from its own clock, and the 250ms tick in the renderer is the only thing that publishes a reading. The invariant is estimate-then-correct. Streamed reply text credits OUTPUT at once from `estimateTokenCount()`. A provider usage report then credits `max(0, real - estimate)` and zeroes the estimate, so no rate goes negative. An overshoot is never clawed back, because OpenCode and OMP report `outputTokens` without the reasoning they stream. Trap 1: INPUT comes only from usage reports, as uncached input plus cache writes. Cache READS are excluded on purpose. They re-read context the provider already holds, so counting them pins the needle on every resumed turn. Codex and Copilot report reads INSIDE `inputTokens`, so for them the reads are subtracted, not skipped. Trap 2: a provider that re-sends the full reply at turn end would count twice. Once a source has reported usage, reply text is refused, and streamed text is refused for `usageSettleMs` after each report. Refused text is never lost, because the next report credits whatever the estimate missed. Do NOT write a second rate engine: a per-chunk store write or a second clock defeats the single 250ms publish.
- **Tokens per second:** `extractClaudeSpeedSamples()`, `computeClaudeTokenSpeed()`, `CodexTokenSpeedTracker`, `TokenSpeedBucketer`, `summarizeTokenSpeed()` in `src/shared/tokenSpeed.ts`, shown with `formatTokensPerSecond()` in `src/shared/formatters.ts`. The failure it replaced: the obvious figure, `query_events` tokens divided by turn duration, measures how busy an agent was (a turn includes tool runs, subagent fan-out, and the user reading), and its token columns were session running totals on older rows. Speed is timed PER API CALL from transcript line timestamps: a Claude response runs from the `user`/`assistant` line before its first block to its last block (other line kinds are written at unrelated moments and are skipped), a Codex response from the user message or previous `token_count` to its `token_usage_record`. Calls are stored as per-session hourly per-model buckets on `AgentSessionInfo.speed`; averages are token-weighted. A PEAK is the fastest model-hour MERGED ACROSS SESSIONS with at least `TOKEN_SPEED_PEAK_MIN_BUCKET_MS` of call time. Do NOT make it the fastest single call: short calls carry block-timestamp artifacts (tens of thousands of tok/s), and gating calls by length fixes output but pushes the input peak below the input average, because prompt throughput is highest on short cached calls. Input counts every prompt token including cache reads. A Claude session's subagent transcripts are timed one file at a time, never joined. A derivation change needs `SESSION_INFO_CACHE_VERSION`, `CODEX_SESSION_CACHE_VERSION` and `TOKEN_USAGE_CACHE_VERSION` bumped, or cached sessions keep the old numbers.
- **Usage, cost and message count of a Claude transcript:** `computeClaudeSessionUsage(sessionFilePath, content)` (async), `computeClaudeUsageWithSubagents(content, subagentContents)` and `readClaudeSubagentTranscripts()` / `readClaudeSubagentTranscriptsSync()` in `src/shared/claudeSessionUsage.ts`; `countClaudeTranscriptMessages(content)` in `src/shared/claudeTranscriptMessages.ts`. Three things about a Claude Code transcript each overstated or understated the session browser's totals. (1) One API response is written as one line per content block (thinking, text, each tool_use) and every line repeats the response's `usage`, so `computeClaudeUsageCost` counts a response once by `message.id` + `requestId`; summing lines overstated cost about 1.5x. (2) A subagent's calls are written to `<sessionId>/subagents/agent-*.jsonl` and never appear in the parent, so pricing the parent alone drops Task spend. (3) A line is not a message: tool results come back as `"type":"user"` lines, plus meta, command, compaction and sidechain lines, so counting `"type":"user"|"assistant"` lines overstated messages about 2.3x; a message is a prompt the user sent or one model response. Every Claude stats reader (project stats, global stats, session list, legacy handlers, CLI) routes through these. All the stats caches are fingerprinted on mtime, so a derivation fix needs a version bump, and the per-project and global caches hold ARCHIVED sessions whose transcripts are gone: upgrade them in place with `STATS_CACHE_MIN_UPGRADABLE_VERSION` / `GLOBAL_STATS_CACHE_MIN_UPGRADABLE_VERSION` and `rescaleArchivedEntries()` (counts rounded, money passed as `fractional`), never a plain bump, which erases lifetime history. SSH listings still price the parent only.

## Paths, files, and strings

- **Path utilities:** `truncatePath()`, `getParentDir()`, `truncateCommand()`, `isAbsolutePath()`, `getBasename()`, `joinPath()` in `src/shared/formatters.ts` (`joinPath` is the renderer-safe `path.join`: it uses whichever separator the base path speaks, so Windows and SSH-remote POSIX paths both survive)
- **Worktree path comparisons:** `normalizeWorktreePath(path, posix?)` and `isPathAtOrUnderRoot(path, root)` in `src/shared/worktreePaths.ts`. The normalizer is re-exported by `src/renderer/utils/worktreeDedup.ts` as `normalizePath` for existing callers. Pass `true` for SSH comparisons to preserve literal backslashes and the `/` root; the default retains local separator handling. Prefix checks use POSIX normalization, retain case, and match directory boundaries. Never collapse `..` before remote physical resolution, because it may follow a symlink.
- **Remote worktree aliases:** `resolveWorktreeAliasesRemote(paths, sshRemote)` in `src/main/utils/remote-git.ts` resolves saved child paths and raw Git registry paths through the same batched physical-directory probe. Each alias has an independently NUL-framed resolved, missing, or unresolved status; one inaccessible path cannot corrupt its siblings. A missing result is a physical candidate, not proof that Git removed the registration. `resolveWorktreePathsRemote()` reuses this probe after resolving the configured parent and base. Keep raw registry identities alongside their physical results, and retain prunable registrations without discovering new agents from inaccessible or missing directories.
- **A path the user typed on the CLI:** `resolveCliPath(input)` in `src/cli/utils/parse.ts` - `~` expanded, then made absolute against the CLI's cwd. Every path that crosses the WS bridge must be absolute: the desktop app's cwd is unrelated to the caller's (it is `/` for a Finder launch), so a relative path resolves against the wrong directory. `profiling stop`, `support-package` and `feedback submit --attach` use it; do not add another local `resolveOutputPath`.
- **An image file the user named on the CLI:** `readCliImageFile(input, maxBytes)` in `src/cli/utils/imageFiles.ts` - resolves the path with `resolveCliPath()`, accepts PNG, JPG, GIF and WebP (what a composer paste carries), checks the size, and returns `{ name, dataUrl }`. Its errors name the file, so a bad one fails before anything crosses the bridge. `feedback submit --attach` and `quick-chat send --image` use it; a verb that attaches an image must not read and base64 the file itself.
- **Classifying a file by extension:** `getFileCategory()`, `isPreviewableFile()`, `matchesFileCategory()`, `getFileExtension()` in `src/shared/fileCategories.ts`. One table answers both "can Maestro open this?" and "which bucket is it in?" - `isPreviewableFile` is DERIVED from `getFileCategory`, so a file cannot show up under a filter pill and then refuse to open, nor be openable while invisible under every pill. Audio and video are NOT listed there; they resolve through `getMediaKind()` in `mediaTypes.ts`, which is the single source of truth for what Chromium can decode. Do NOT hand-roll another `TEXT_EXTENSIONS` set - the private copies inside `FileSearchModal` are exactly what this replaced. Distinct from the sets in `src/renderer/utils/fileExplorerIcons/shared.ts`, which exist to pick an ICON and split far more finely (lockfiles, test folders); pick by question and do not merge them.
- **Reading a zip from disk:** `readZipArchive(filePath, options?)`, `extractZipTo()`, `isUnsafeZipEntryName()` in `src/main/utils/zip-archive.ts`. Playbook import, Cue backup inspect/restore, and the debug-package tests only need entry names and bytes. The previous reader was `adm-zip`, whose `extractAllTo(..., overwrite)` follows a destination symlink (GHSA-vwc7-r8mq-g2x9) and has no patched release. A wrapper around that API cannot close Dependabot, and `npm audit fix --force` would downgrade to 0.5.8 and re-open the zip-bomb advisory fixed in 0.6.0. `unzipSync` inflates every selected entry, so a caller that only needs `manifest.json` must pass `names` or `filter` (Cue list/inspect do). Default caps on entry count and aggregate `originalSize` refuse a zip bomb before the bytes sit in the main process. `extractZipTo` refuses `..` / absolute names, refuses to overwrite a dest that is already a symlink, and walks every existing path component under the dest root so an intermediate symlink cannot escape. Do NOT add a second unzip helper in a handler or test; `analyze-perf-trace.mjs` is the one exception (it cannot import compiled main-process code) and it still uses `fflate.unzipSync` rather than extract-to-disk.
- **Guarding a serialized JSON payload before it overwrites a file:** `assertSerializedJsonIsSafe(serialized, target)` in `src/shared/jsonUtils.ts`. The failure it exists to stop is `JSON.stringify` returning `undefined` - which it does for `undefined`, a function, or a symbol - because `writeFile` then persists the literal text `"undefined"`: unparseable, and a silent way to clobber a good store. The empty string is rejected for the same reason. It does NOT unconditionally round-trip parse. Beyond the `undefined` case `JSON.stringify` cannot produce malformed JSON (a circular reference throws instead of emitting something broken), so the parse only ever catches truncation, and a string still in memory cannot be truncated. Above 1MB it therefore falls back to a balanced-delimiter check on the first and last non-whitespace characters. That matters because the check is paid on the write path: on the 20MB agent-runs store the re-parse alone cost ~40ms per write, on the Electron main thread, which is the same thread that answers every IPC call - and a field trace attributed 82% of active main-process CPU to synchronous JSON work of exactly this shape. Two rival copies existed before this was hoisted: a private `assertSerializedJsonIsSafe` in `src/main/utils/atomic-json-store.ts` and a bare, unexplained `JSON.parse(content)` inline in `atomicWriteJson` in `src/cli/services/agent-run-store.ts`. Do NOT add a third, and do NOT "harden" it back into an unconditional parse - the large-payload branch is the whole point, not an oversight. The trailing-whitespace tolerance is load-bearing too: the agent-run store appends a newline to every file it writes.
- **Deterministic JSON (same value, same text):** `canonicalJson(value)` in `src/shared/canonicalJson.ts`. `JSON.stringify` writes keys in insertion order, so two objects holding the same data can serialize differently - harmless in storage, wrong for anything that hashes or compares the text (a content hash, a cache key, an equality check, a payload that gets signed). This sorts keys by UTF-16 code unit at every depth, keeps arrays in order, writes no whitespace, and leaves out an object member whose value is `undefined`. Those are the rules of RFC 8785 (JCS), so a reader in another language can reproduce the bytes. It THROWS on anything that is not a JSON value (`NaN` / `Infinity`, `undefined` outside an object member, a bigint, a function, a `Date` / `Map` / class instance, a circular reference) where `JSON.stringify` would quietly write `null` or `{}`: a canonical form that changes a value on the way out makes two different values compare equal. Two traps a hand-rolled copy falls into: `JSON.stringify(value, Object.keys(value).sort())` treats the array as an allowlist at EVERY depth, so nested keys that are not also top-level keys are silently dropped; and copying into a new object with sorted keys does not sort it, because an engine lists integer-like keys first, in numeric order, whatever order they were added in. Do NOT sort with `localeCompare` - the result then depends on the machine's locale.
- **Redacting secrets from text you persist or show:** `redactSecrets(text, opts?)`, `passesLuhn()` in `src/shared/redactSecrets.ts`. Returns `{ text, redacted }` with typed placeholders (`[REDACTED_API_KEY]`, `[REDACTED_AWS_ACCESS_KEY]`, `[REDACTED_BEARER_TOKEN]`, `[REDACTED_CREDIT_CARD]`, `[REDACTED_SECRET]`). It replaced three disagreeing regex lists: the agent-run ledger knew `AKIA` but not `gho_`, Send Feedback knew prefixed env names (`OPENAI_API_KEY=`) but not passwords, and none knew card numbers (13-19 digits, Luhn-checked so order ids survive), JWTs, or PEM private keys. Key=value and `Bearer` matches keep their key so the text still reads. Options instead of copies: `placeholder` replaces the whole match with one opaque string (the agent-run ledger's `[redacted]`), `labels` relabels a kind (Send Feedback's `[REDACTED_GITHUB_TOKEN]`), and `hexBlobs` opts into 40+ hex runs, which is OFF by default because a git SHA is exactly 40 hex characters and a recall store (Computer History) must keep commit ids. URLs: `redactUrl()` in `src/shared/computer-history/sanitize.ts` composes it per query parameter.
- **Strip ANSI / collapse progress overwrites:** `stripAnsiCodes()`, `processCarriageReturns()` in `src/shared/stringUtils.ts`
- **Matching error patterns against an agent's exit output:** `stripJsonEventLines()` in `src/shared/agentErrorPatterns.ts`. A parser's `detectErrorFromExit` scans stderr plus stdout with the pattern bank, but a structured (JSONL) stdout was already classified one event at a time as it streamed. A JSON event is one physical line, so a loose pattern like `/token.*limit/` matches across a whole assistant message: a turn that exited non-zero after quoting "token" and later "limit" was reported as "Context length exceeded" at 25% context. Every parser (Claude, Codex, Copilot, OpenCode, Factory Droid, Grok, Antigravity) passes stdout through it before matching; plain-text lines between events still match. Do NOT drop stdout from the exit scan instead: a CLI that prints its reason as plain text on stdout would then go unclassified.
- **Reading what a TUI painted from a raw capture:** `replayTerminalScreen(raw)`, `capturedAlternateScreen(raw)` in `src/maestro-p/screen-replay.ts`. A diffing renderer (Claude Code's alternate-screen UI) rewrites only the cells that changed and jumps the cursor over the rest, so `stripAnsiCodes` on a captured stream loses text that is still on screen: the `/usage` scrape read the second weekly header as a bare `Fable)` and reported that bar as 0%. Replay puts every cell where the terminal drew it. Strip is right for line-oriented output you only need clean; replay is right whenever the capture is cursor-addressed. It deliberately has no wrapping or scrolling, so it needs no PTY size, and it returns the LAST alternate screen even after the TUI switched back out of it.
- **Loading a LOCAL file tree:** `walkLocalFileTree()` in `src/main/utils/file-tree-walk.ts`, reached from the renderer as `window.maestro.fs.readDirTree` and wrapped by `loadFileTree()` in `src/renderer/utils/fileExplorer.ts`. The whole walk happens in ONE round-trip. Do NOT recurse with `window.maestro.fs.readDir` from the renderer: that costs an IPC round-trip per directory, and on a large tree those round-trips - not the disk - are the entire load time, because each one waits its turn on a renderer main thread that may be busy rendering a streaming transcript. A vault worth about 100ms of real filesystem work sat on "Loading files..." for minutes, while the footer counts appeared immediately because `fs:directorySize` had always walked the tree in a single main-process call. SSH trees are the exception and keep the renderer walk, since a remote tree is batched separately through `listTreeRemote`. The traversal rules (ignore patterns, optional root `.gitignore`, depth cap, entry cap that folders are exempt from, and an always-visible `.maestro` subtree loaded in full past the cap) live in the walker, so a rule added on one side only is a rule local and SSH trees disagree about.
- **Telling the Files panel a file appeared or vanished:** `requestFileTreeRefresh(sessionId)` in `src/renderer/utils/fileTreeRefresh.ts` (renderer) and `nudgeFileTreeForPaths(paths)` in `src/cli/services/file-tree-refresh.ts` (CLI). The panel refreshes on a TIMER, so anything that writes or deletes outside that cadence leaves the tree describing a directory that no longer exists, and the user reads that as the save having done nothing. Both ends raise the same `maestro:refreshFileTree` event, whose single app-level listener lives in `useAppRemoteEventListeners` - which is the point: a deeply-nested hook nudges the tree without anyone prop-drilling `refreshFileTree` down to it. Do NOT hand-roll another `new CustomEvent('maestro:refreshFileTree', ...)`; there were four copies with four different comments. Fire it from the function that DID the write (`saveImageToProject` does), not from each caller, so a new surface is covered the moment it renders. The CLI half is deliberately silent and never throws: the bytes are already on disk, so a closed desktop must not turn a good write into a failed command - the loud form is `refreshFileTreeFor()`, behind `maestro-cli refresh-files`, where the refresh IS the command.
- **Delete the file the user is previewing:** `requestFileDeletion({ path, sshRemoteId?, sessionId? })` in `src/renderer/services/fileDeletion.ts`. Opens the shared destructive `confirm` modal, deletes through `window.maestro.fs.delete` (SSH-aware, same IPC the Files panel uses), force-closes every preview tab pointing at the path, and nudges the Files panel through `requestFileTreeRefresh()`. Both the File Preview toolbar's trash button and the command palette's `File: Delete` entry route through it - do NOT hand-roll a second confirm-then-delete path, or the two surfaces drift on what they warn about and what they clean up. See [RENDERER-SERVICES.md](RENDERER-SERVICES.md#filedeletionts---delete-the-previewed-file).

## Main process: stores, persistence, platform

- **One log entry as one JSON line (`--log-format json`):** `formatJsonLogLine()` / `buildJsonLogLine()` / `JSON_LOG_ID_FIELDS` in `src/shared/jsonLogLine.ts`. Both log paths of a headless Cue engine render through it - the engine's onLog (`jsonCueLog` in `src/cli/services/cue-standalone-engine.ts`) and the main-process `logger` in its `consoleJson()` mode - so field names cannot drift between the engine's lines and the shared modules' lines. Only the allowlisted identifier fields (`runId`, `subscriptionName`, `pipelineId`, `sessionId`, `status`, plus `type` as `event`) are lifted from `data`; the payload is never copied, because a structured log is exactly what gets shipped off the box. A new field reaches the line by being added to the allowlist on purpose.
- **A CLI verb that needs the desktop app, when the app is absent:** `exitIfMaestroNotRunning(error, options)` and `failFromError(error, json)` in `src/cli/services/session-command.ts`, over `MaestroNotRunningError` / `MAESTRO_NOT_RUNNING_MESSAGE` / `MAESTRO_NOT_RUNNING_CODE` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/client/not-running.ts` (re-exported for the CLI by `src/cli/services/maestro-not-running.ts`). The library's `BridgeConnection.connect()` (`MaestroClient` in the CLI) throws the typed error for every way the app can be absent (no discovery file, a stale pid, a socket that never opened, one refused with an errno), and `exitCodeForError()` maps it to `ExitCode.NotRunning`. The message is a contract (CLI-HEADLESS.md) and never changes; the next step for a person (start the desktop app, or `maestro-cli host start`) rides the error's `hint`, which the human report prints on a second line. The failure it replaced: verbs caught the connect error and flattened it to a string (`failCommand(error.message)`, `Failed to X: ${msg}`) before anything could map it, so the same condition exited 1 under five spellings, and four verbs (`dispatch`, `session`, `queue`, `gist`) each kept a private English-substring classifier that missed the connect timeout and misfiled any desktop error that happened to say "websocket" or "not running". Put `exitIfMaestroNotRunning` FIRST in any catch that wraps a bridge call; for everything else it returns, so the site's own handling is untouched. It keeps a verb's envelope (`jsonExtra`, e.g. `{ type: 'error' }`) and stream (`stderrJson`) while making the message, code and exit uniform. Two traps: an HTTP rejection of the upgrade (the Web Login gate's 401) is NOT this error - the app answered, so it is running - and the error lives in its own module rather than the client facade `maestro-client.ts` because dozens of tests replace that module with a factory mock, which would otherwise erase the class and break `instanceof`. Do NOT match on the message text and do NOT add another `mapTransportError`.
- **An explicit data directory on a CLI verb (`--data-dir`):** `applyDataDirOption(dataDir)`, `requireDataDirOrExit({ json, log })`, `describeDataDirSource()` in `src/cli/services/data-dir-option.ts`. The flag is applied by WRITING `MAESTRO_USER_DATA` (resolved with `resolveCliPath()`), not by threading a path through readers: every reader already resolves through `resolveUserDataDir()`, so one assignment before anything touches disk reaches the engine lock, `cue.db`, the trigger inbox, the session readers, the desktop discovery file and the electron shim at once, and the engine's children inherit it (server mode keeps `MAESTRO_*`). The flag beats an inherited value. `requireDataDirOrExit` is the `DATA_DIR_NOT_FOUND` refusal the `cue engine` verbs and `cue trigger --data-dir` share; an explicit path is refused, never created. Do NOT add a `dataDir` parameter to a reader instead - two ways to name the folder is how readers disagree about it.
- **Reading or writing `maestro-agent-configs.json` without Electron:** `readAgentConfigsStoreFile(dataDir)` and `writeAgentConfigsStoreFile(dataDir, data)` in `src/main/stores/agent-configs-store-file.ts`. The CLI (`readAgentConfigs()` and the read half of `writeAgentConfigValue()` in `src/cli/services/storage.ts`), the bundle exporter (provider env) and the bundle importer (`--agent-path`) all read through it. The file is keyed by PROVIDER (`configs['claude-code']`), not by agent: `configs[toolType].customPath` is the binary Cue's run router, the standalone engine, the CLI spawner and the desktop's agent detector all launch, and Cue never reads an agent record's own `customPath`. Content that is not a JSON object throws `AgentConfigsStoreCorruptError` rather than reading as empty. The desktop keeps this store in its PRODUCTION data path even in dev mode (`stores/instances.ts`).
- **Reading or writing `maestro-sessions.json` without Electron:** `readSessionsStoreFile(dataDir)` and `writeSessionsStoreFile(dataDir, data)` in `src/main/stores/sessions-store-file.ts`. The CLI (`readSessions()` in `src/cli/services/storage.ts`), the bundle exporter and the bundle importer all go through it, so there is one reader that takes an explicit data dir instead of one per caller. A missing file reads as empty; content that is not a JSON object throws `SessionsStoreCorruptError` instead of reading as "no agents", because a caller that then writes would replace every agent with its own short list. The writer keeps every top-level key it is handed (`activeSessionId` and anything newer), so read the whole `data` and spread it. Only write while the desktop is NOT running against that data dir: the desktop serves this file from an in-memory cache (`deferStoreWrites`) and its next flush overwrites an external edit.
- **Maestro's data directory, without Electron:** `resolveUserDataDir(options?)`, `assertUserDataDirExists(dir)` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/paths/userDataDir.ts` (re-exported at `src/shared/userDataDir.ts`). Anything with Electron should keep calling `app.getPath('userData')`; this is for what cannot - the CLI and the standalone Cue runner - and it must land on the SAME directory or the two disagree about what state exists. `MAESTRO_USER_DATA` wins, because the app publishes its resolved path into the environment at startup (`src/main/index.ts`), which is what makes demo mode and the dev redirect work for child processes without special cases. The fallback is the platform root plus the app name, and the name is NOT constant: Electron derives it from `package.json` `name` (`maestro`) unpackaged and from `build.productName` (`Maestro`) once packaged, with development redirecting to a sibling `maestro-dev` unless `USE_PROD_DATA` is set. A process with no Electron cannot tell those apart, so it assumes the installed spelling and a standalone runner must call `assertUserDataDirExists` rather than open whatever is at the computed path - creating it silently would put an empty database beside the real one. The distinction only bites on a case-sensitive filesystem; macOS folds `Maestro` and `maestro` together. The discovery file (`getConfigDir()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/client/discovery.ts`, re-exported at `src/shared/cli-server-discovery.ts`) and `src/shared/cli-activity.ts` used to hard-code the lowercase name and now resolve through this function; do not add another fallback.
- **Where the synced stores live (custom Storage Location):** `resolveSyncDir(userDataDir)`, `isValidSyncPath()`, `BOOTSTRAP_STORE_FILE` in `src/shared/syncPath.ts`; the CLI's `getSyncDir()` in `src/cli/services/storage.ts`. Settings -> General -> Storage Location moves settings, agents, groups, session origins, pasted images and group chats out of the user-data directory, and records the choice in `maestro-bootstrap.json`, which stays behind. A process without Electron has to read that file to find the rest: the CLI used to read every store from the user-data directory alone, so it listed only the agents that existed before the move and answered `Agent not found` for every one created since. Two directories, not one: history, playbooks, agent configs and `cli-server.json` never move, so do NOT point `MAESTRO_USER_DATA` at the sync folder - the CLI then finds the agents and loses the running app. The validator is shared with the desktop's `getCustomSyncPath()` because the app silently falls back to the user-data directory for a path it rejects, and a reader that honored the path anyway would be reading files the app is not writing.
- **Deferred main-process store persistence:** `deferStoreWrites(store, memoKey)` in `src/main/stores/deferred-writes.ts`, wired for sessions by `flushPendingSessionWrites()` / `flushPendingSessionWritesSync()` in `src/main/stores/instances.ts`. This is the canonical path for a large, hot `electron-store` document whose synchronous read-parse-stringify-write cycle would block Electron's main thread. It owns the read-through cache, per-array-element serialization memo, coalescing timer, ordered atomic-write drain, recoverable-error retry, and shutdown flush. **Do not wrap another store casually:** caching is safe only when Maestro's main process is the sole writer and no file watcher expects external edits. Mutations must replace memoized array elements immutably; changing one in place deliberately reuses its old serialization. Async commits are revision-ordered, and the final rename is synchronous so `flushSync()` can invalidate an older temp write without that write later replacing the shutdown snapshot. IPC handlers that promise persistence must await `flushPendingSessionWrites()` before acknowledging success; acknowledging the cache update would advance the renderer baseline even if disk later returns `EIO` or `EACCES`. The synchronous form is for shutdown only, where the process is about to be hard-exited.
- **Rewriting a large `{ key: [...] }` JSON store file:** `serializeWithMemoizedArray(data, memoKey)` in `src/shared/memoizedJsonSerialize.ts`. Byte-identical to `JSON.stringify(data, undefined, '\t')`, but each array element's JSON is memoized by OBJECT IDENTITY, so a write pays only for the elements that changed. Used by `deferStoreWrites()` for `maestro-sessions.json` and by the CLI agent-run store for the runs ledger (shared/ because the CLI bundle cannot import main-process modules). **The contract is immutability:** an element mutated in place keeps its stale memo and is written out stale; replace changed elements with new objects. The agent-run store pairs it with a stat-signature read cache (inode, size, mtime in ns) so a write by another process is still picked up; copy that pattern, not a plain read-through cache, for any file maestro-cli also writes.
- **Recovering from a store file that is not JSON:** `createStoreDeserializer(storePath)` and `corruptStorePath(storePath, now?)` in `src/main/stores/corrupt-store-recovery.ts`, wired into every `new Store(...)` by `deserializeStoreJson(name, cwd)` in `src/main/stores/instances.ts`. conf calls the `deserialize` hook from inside its own `store` getter, which runs in the Store CONSTRUCTOR and again on every `get`/`set`, and it rethrows a `SyntaxError` unless `clearInvalidConfig` is set. So a bare `JSON.parse` there means one torn file bricks the app permanently: `initializeStores()` throws before a window exists, relaunching re-reads the same bytes, and nothing on the restart path repairs it. A field crash showed an 8 MB `maestro-sessions.json` truncated mid-write reporting the same unhandled `SyntaxError` hundreds of times in three hours. The recovery moves the file to a stamped `.corrupt-<stamp>.json` sidecar and returns an empty document so conf applies the store's defaults and startup continues. Do NOT reach for conf's `clearInvalidConfig` instead: it discards the document silently, and this file is the only copy of every agent, tab and transcript the user has. Do NOT delete the file or let the next write overwrite it, for the same reason - the stamp exists so a second incident cannot land on the first quarantine. Only a `SyntaxError` is recovered; anything else rethrows, because an unreadable disk is not something to paper over. Atomic writes (`stores/deferred-writes.ts`) remove OUR hand as a cause but not the others: `syncPath` is user-configurable and routinely a cloud-sync folder that truncates and half-materializes files on its own schedule.
- **Sealing a file with the key the OS holds for the app:** `safeStorageSeal(safeStorage)` and the `SealProvider` / `SafeStorageLike` types in `src/main/utils/safe-storage-seal.ts`. Electron's `safeStorage` encrypts with a key the OS keeps for the app (Keychain on macOS, DPAPI on Windows, a keyring on Linux), so a sealed file cannot be read by something that only reads files. The definitions started inside `src/main/plugins/authorization-ledger.ts`, which still re-exports them; they live in `utils/` so a store that seals its own file does not grow a private copy. Three things a caller must plan for. `safeStorage` works only after the app's `ready` event, so build the provider whenever you like but do not call it earlier. `available()` can be false (a Linux machine with no keyring), and it never throws: the caller decides what it does without a seal, and must not assume one. A blob sealed under another key does not open (another install, a reset keychain, a development build reading the installed app's file), so `unseal` can throw on a file that was readable yesterday - handle that as an unreadable file, and do not write over bytes another start may be able to read. Take the `SealProvider` as an injected dependency rather than importing `safeStorage` at the call site, so the unavailable and unreadable paths can be tested with a fake. Do NOT write another `isEncryptionAvailable()` wrapper. `obscuringSeal()` in the same file is the fallback for a machine where `available()` is false: a `SealProvider` that is always available, under a key built into the app (AES-256-GCM, a new IV per seal). It keeps a file from ever being written as readable text, so a search of the disk does not turn up its content and a hand edit fails to open, but it is NOT secrecy from anything that can read the app. Reach for it instead of writing plain JSON, and tell the person the file is obscured rather than encrypted.
- **Proving a sealed file is the newest one (rollback and deletion):** `createKeyringAnchor(service, account)`, the `Anchor` / `AnchorStore` types, `keyringAnchor(entryFactory)` and `noAnchor()` in `src/main/utils/keyring-anchor.ts`. A sealed file proves its contents were not edited. It does not prove it is the NEWEST file: an older sealed copy put back in its place opens just as well, and a deleted file reads exactly like a first run. An anchor is a few bytes kept OUTSIDE the data folder, in the OS credential store (Keychain, Credential Manager, a Linux keyring), that the owning store compares its file against: `installSecret` binds the file to this anchor and `epoch` counts the changes that matter, so a file whose epoch is behind is an older copy and a missing file with a live anchor was removed. The definitions started inside `src/main/plugins/authorization-ledger.ts`, which still re-exports them; they live in `utils/` so a second store that needs the same guarantee does not grow a private keyring wrapper. Four things a caller must plan for. Take the `AnchorStore` as an injected dependency so the store is testable with a fake and no test ever writes to the developer's real keychain. `read` and `write` are SYNCHRONOUS calls into the OS, so keep them off hot paths and off startup where possible. `write` can throw (a locked credential store) while `available()`, `read()` and `clear()` do not, and the native module is loaded lazily so a machine without it gets `available() === false` rather than a crash. Decide the write order on purpose: writing the anchor first means a failed file write leaves the anchor AHEAD (reads as a rollback), writing the file first means it leaves the anchor BEHIND, so a store that writes file-first must treat only a file that is behind as stale. Give each data folder its own account name (development, demo and installed runs each have their own folder; one shared entry makes each call the others' files stale). It is friction, not a wall: a process running as the same OS user can delete the entry, so do not state a stronger guarantee than that.
- **Writing a Claude session's origin record:** `setClaudeSessionOrigin(store, projectPath, agentSessionId, patch)` and `mergeClaudeSessionOrigin()` in `src/main/storage/claude-session-origins.ts`. The record in `maestro-claude-session-origins.json` holds several independent fields (origin, tab name, star, last context usage) written by independent paths, so every writer must MERGE its patch into the record rather than replace it. `claude:registerSessionOrigin` fires on every turn and used to replace the record with the bare origin string; that silently erased a third of all saved tab names, and those sessions disappeared from the Tab Switcher's "All Named" list and lost their titles in the Claude sessions browser, even though history still showed the names. `backfillClaudeSessionNames()` in the same file (run once by `stores/migrations/claude-session-names-backfill.ts`) restores lost names from the `sessionName` history stamps on each entry. Do NOT write `origins[projectPath][id] = ...` by hand at a new call site, and do NOT add a field to the record through anything but this helper.
- **Serializing async work on one entity:** `createKeyedWriteQueue()` in `src/shared/keyedWriteQueue.ts`, re-exported from `src/main/utils/atomic-json-store.ts` so all seven main-process call sites keep their existing import. Each key (a session id, a `${sessionId}:${tabId}` pair) gets its own promise chain: callers mutating the same entity run strictly one at a time while different keys stay concurrent, and the chain continues past a rejection so one failed write cannot strand every later write for that key. Settled entries are deleted from the backing Map, which is what keeps it bounded in a process that runs for days. It lives in `shared/` rather than beside `atomicWriteJson` because BOTH processes need it and the renderer cannot import a module that pulls in `fs/promises` - the same hoist, for the same reason, as `assertSerializedJsonIsSafe` in `src/shared/jsonUtils.ts`.

  **A queue held in one process cannot order work performed in another.** A remote tab rename is therefore ordered twice, and the two queues answer different questions: the one in `src/main/web-server/callbacks/tabCallbacks.ts` orders the PROTOCOL - it is what puts `rename_tab_result` frames back on the wire in request order - while the one in `src/renderer/hooks/remote/useRemoteIntegration.ts` orders the WORK, which is where a rename actually happens (provider metadata write, history relabel, store patch). The server's queue deliberately stops waiting after `RENAME_CONFIRMATION_RELEASE_MS`, so an unresponsive renderer cannot wedge a tab; at that moment it frees the slot and dispatches the next rename while the abandoned one may still be running in the renderer. Those two then overlap and the older one can finish last, overwriting the newer name in both the provider metadata and the store - the exact lost update the queue was added to prevent, reopened past the release window. Both queues key on `${sessionId}:${tabId}`, so ordering is per tab and a rename never becomes an app-wide lock.

  Three rules for a renderer-side queue. It must be at MODULE scope, never a ref: the listener is registered from an effect that can re-run, and a queue rebuilt on re-registration forgets the work still in flight (`group-chat-storage.ts` holds its own at module scope for the same reason). Its slot must be handed on after a bounded budget as well as on settle, because `ipcRenderer.invoke` never times out - a main-process handler that never returns leaves its promise pending for the life of the window, and every later rename for that tab would sit behind it unattempted. And handing the slot on early is not cancellation: an in-flight IPC cannot be recalled, so the writer re-reads the newest REQUESTED name when its own write lands and writes once more if it was superseded, which is what turns a stale write that lands late into a self-correcting one instead of the final word. Do NOT hand-roll another `Map<string, Promise<void>>` chain.

- **Capping stored tool output:** `MAX_PERSISTED_TOOL_OUTPUT_CHARS`, `compactToolOutput()` and `compactSessionToolOutputs()` in `src/shared/toolOutput.ts`. A tool result is transcript evidence, but it is also copied into the renderer store, `maestro-sessions.json`, and the entire `sessions:getAll` web bootstrap. One Antigravity command stored a 67MB one-line result, which grew the bootstrap frame to 126MB and made iOS appear disconnected while it parsed the same Maestro instance. Cap at parser ingestion to stop new growth, at renderer persistence for every provider, and on main-process load to heal old files before any browser receives them. Short values retain their original type; oversized objects become a JSON preview because preserving a large object defeats the cap. Do not add another provider-specific limit or remove the load migration: historical sessions survive parser fixes until a storage boundary rewrites them.
- **Merging browser-deferred conversations:** `projectWebSession()`, `readDeferredContent()`, and `mergeDeferredSessionContent()` in `src/main/stores/deferred-session-content.ts` (pure; the persistence handlers own the store), over `mergeDeferredItems()` and `MAX_PERSISTED_SESSION_LOGS` in `src/shared/deferredSessionContent.ts`. `sessions:getBootstrap` omits AI tab logs, legacy session logs, and command history so a phone does not parse the whole sessions file at startup. The `deferredContent` marker follows that browser record through the renderer store. On either write path (`sessions:setMany` and `sessions:setAll`), main merges untouched fields from its full stored record, then removes the marker before saving. A selected tab and legacy shell output load through `sessions:getDeferredContent`; merging by log id keeps entries that arrived while the read was in flight. A stale browser tab already closed by another client is dropped without blocking the rest of the save. Do not persist a projected record without this merge, or a routine browser save erases every unloaded conversation. Every Retry control (failed agent read, unloaded conversation) raises `requestWebBridgeReconcile()` from `src/renderer/services/webBridgeReconcile.ts`; `useSessionRestoration` is the one listener.
- **HTTP request from the main process:** `fetchWithTimeout(url, options?, timeoutMs?)` in `src/main/utils/fetchWithTimeout.ts`, with `isFetchTimeoutError()` and `DEFAULT_FETCH_TIMEOUT_MS`. A bare `fetch()` has no timeout, so a stalled socket hangs the caller forever: in the main process that is an IPC handler that never settles and a renderer spinner that never stops. Do NOT hand-roll `new AbortController()` + `setTimeout`, and do NOT add another local `fetchWithTimeout` - there used to be three functions with that exact name and three different signatures. It composes a caller-supplied `signal` rather than overwriting it, so passing your own cancellation still works. Callers needing extra behaviour wrap it locally (see `fetchBmadResource()` in `src/main/bmad-manager.ts`).
- **A git query the UI polls in the background:** `execGitReadOnly()`, `isGitTimeout()`, `READ_ONLY_GIT_TIMEOUT_MS` in `src/main/utils/remote-git.ts`. Anything that runs git on a timer (the git indicator's `git:status`, `git:numstat`, `git:branch` and `git:info`) goes through it rather than `execGit()`. A repo in an iCloud Drive or other file-provider folder blocks every `git status` while the OS downloads offloaded files, and a bare `execGit()` has no timeout and no in-flight guard, so each poll tick added one more stuck process - a reporter killed 124 of them (#1739). It does three things: sets `GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0` so a background read never takes `.git/index.lock` from the agent's own git (the env var, not `--no-optional-locks`, because the flag is a hard error before git 2.15 and an older SSH host would turn it into an empty status that reads as clean); kills the child (local `git` or the `ssh` invocation) after the timeout; and single-flights per SSH remote + folder + argument list, so a caller arriving mid-query joins it. Each caller settles by its own deadline, but the in-flight entry is held until the child actually EXITS, so a process that ignores its kill signal still blocks new spawns: the worst case is one process per folder per query, never one per tick. A timeout comes back as `exitCode: 'ETIMEDOUT'` with empty stdout, which is NOT an answer - the handlers add `timedOut: true` to the reply and the renderer keeps its last good value for that folder rather than showing a clean tree. Killing the local `ssh` does not reach a git already running on the remote host. One-shot reads the user is waiting on (diff, log, show) stay on `execGit()`.
- **SSH remote lookup:** `getSshRemoteById()` in `src/main/stores/getters.ts`
- **Platform detection:** `isWindows()`, `isMacOS()`, `isLinux()` in `src/shared/platformDetection.ts` (works in both processes). In renderer-only code prefer `isWindowsPlatform()`, `isMacOSPlatform()`, `isLinuxPlatform()` in `src/renderer/utils/platformUtils.ts`. Never read `process.platform` in the renderer: the `process` polyfill (`src/renderer/public/process-shim.js`) reports the sentinel `'browser'`, and `navigator.platform` is deprecated and unreliable. Those helpers describe the CLIENT. To ask what the machine Maestro runs on can install or run (a macOS-only feature, for example), use `hostPlatform()` in the same file: in a web-desktop browser the client is some other device, so it answers `''` and a platform-restricted first-party feature is not offered there (`isFirstPartyPluginAvailableOn()` in `src/shared/plugins/first-party.ts`).
- **Encore Feature flags and their defaults:** `ENCORE_FEATURE_DEFAULTS`, `resolveEncoreFeatures(raw)` in `src/shared/encoreFeatureDefaults.ts`. Four processes read these flags (the renderer store, `SETTINGS_METADATA`, the main-process Cue/stats gates, and `maestro-cli encore`), and each one used to carry its own copy of the defaults while treating a missing key as `false`. That is not a cosmetic duplicate: a feature that ships ON reads OFF in the main process until something persists the object, so on a fresh install the UI showed Cue running while the stats lineage writer and the telemetry gate for the same run were silently disabled. Resolve every read through `resolveEncoreFeatures()` - it merges onto the defaults and only lets a real boolean override one, so a settings file written before a flag existed keeps that flag's default instead of falling to `undefined` -> false. Its keys are derived from the defaults object, never hand-listed, so a newly graduated feature cannot be missed by the merge. A capability ships off (a plugin) and its entry here flips to `true` when it graduates to an Encore Feature; do NOT re-declare the literal anywhere else. A handler gate reads one flag with `isEncoreFeatureEnabled(settingsStore, flag)` and refuses with `requireEncoreFeature(settingsStore, flag, 'XDisabled')`, never `ef.x === true` or `ef.x !== false` on the raw object. A feature's background services (watchers, timers, sockets, child processes) start and stop through `createEncoreFeatureLifecycle()` in `src/main/encore-feature-lifecycle.ts`, driven by the one `encoreFeatures` store change: `maestro-cli encore enable|disable` is a plain settings write that never reaches the first-party bridge, so a per-feature listener or a bridge-only stop leaves a CLI-disabled feature running until relaunch.
- **Whether a stored agent is live (a switched-off Pianola is dormant):** `filterLiveAgents(agents, encoreFeatures)`, `isAgentDormant(agent, encoreFeatures)`, `isPianolaEnabled()`, `DORMANT_PIANOLA_MESSAGE` in `src/shared/agentVisibility.ts`, bound per process as `getLiveSessions()` (main, `src/main/stores/getters.ts`), `readLiveSessions()` (CLI, `src/cli/services/storage.ts`), and `useLiveSessions()` / `getLiveSessionsSnapshot()` (renderer, `src/renderer/hooks/session/useLiveSessions.ts`). The Pianola agent is a real `isPianola` Session that stays in the store after its Encore flag is switched off, so turning the feature back on restores it. Every surface that read the raw session list kept acting on it while the feature was off: its home-folder `cwd` blocked unrelated folders, name resolution in `send` / `dispatch` / `ask` still found it, Cue watched it, group chat offered it for @mention, Director's Notes summarized it, and git polled its folder. Anything that lists, resolves, watches, polls, or counts agents goes through these helpers (both accept the raw or resolved `encoreFeatures`, and `filterLiveAgents()` returns the same array when nothing is dormant). Persistence, store migrations, and pruning that keys off the full list must keep reading the raw store, or they would delete the dormant agent's data. The renderer's `sessionVisibility.ts` (`filterSessionsVisibleInSidebar()`) delegates to the same rule.
- **A maestro-cli verb that belongs to an Encore Feature:** `encoreCommand(parent, name, flag, flags)`, `requireEncore(flag, json?)`, `requireEncoreForOptions(flag, options, owned)`, `allowWhileEncoreOff(command)` in `src/cli/utils/encore-gate.ts`. A switched-off feature has no footprint in the CLI: `encoreCommand` registers the verb hidden (absent from `--help`, `reference` and `completions`) and adds a preAction hook that refuses every verb beneath it, invoked directly or not, with the one line that turns it back on (`<Name> is turned off. Turn it on in Settings > Plugins, or run: maestro-cli encore enable <flag>`; `--json` gets `<FLAG>_DISABLED`). The hook applies the verb's own `--data-dir` first, so a headless data directory is judged by its own settings. `requireEncoreForOptions` covers a core verb whose options belong to a feature (Groups+ on `create-group --parent`). Before this, each feature carried its own check: Pianola's told the user to run a command that does not exist, Director's Notes read a never-saved flag as off although it ships on, and Computer History reads warned and carried on. Read flags through `readEncoreFlags()` (never `flags?.x === true`), keep `encore` itself ungated (it is how a hidden feature is turned on), and reserve `allowWhileEncoreOff` for a verb that only takes a footprint away, such as `cue engine stop`. `reference --all` (what `npm run gen:cli-reference` runs) still documents every verb.
- **Filing GitHub feedback (check gh, search, +1, submit):** the service functions in `src/main/feedback/index.ts` (`checkFeedbackGhAuth`, `searchFeedbackIssues`, `subscribeFeedbackIssue`, `submitFeedbackConversation`, `buildFeedbackConversationPrompt`). The `feedback:*` IPC handlers and the WS bridge (`maestro-cli feedback`) are both thin wrappers over them, so the CLI files the byte-identical issue the Send Feedback modal does. Wire contract, category aliases and attachment limits are in `src/shared/feedback.ts`. Do NOT put feedback logic back inside `ipcMain.handle` bodies - that is what made it unreachable from the CLI.

## Spawning and stopping agents (process, SSH, env, prompts)

- **A secret a server supplies by name:** `lookupSecret(name, options?)`, `resolveSecrets(names, options?)`, `isValidSecretName()`, `describeSecretProblem()` in `src/shared/serverSecrets.ts`. ONE lookup for every place a server secret is read: Cue and CLI agent launches (`buildAgentLaunchPlan`'s `requiredSecrets`, scoped to the agent record's `requiredSecrets`), webhook `secret_env`, and the `bundle import` secrets report. Order is `$CREDENTIALS_DIRECTORY/<NAME>` (systemd), then `/run/secrets/<NAME>` (Docker/Kubernetes), then the environment: a file is the deliberate channel and must not be shadowed by a stale env value, and a file that EXISTS but is unusable (unreadable, a directory, empty, over 64 KiB) is reported rather than falling through. Names must be env var names, which is also the traversal guard; one trailing line ending is dropped. Values go into one child's environment and nowhere else - never `process.env` (that hands every secret to every agent), never the plan's `envVars` (shown in Process Details, crosses to SSH), never a log, error message, database row or status payload; every report it returns names and paths only. Do NOT read `process.env[secretName]` directly for a server secret, and do NOT add a second file lookup: the readiness gate and anything else that asks "is this secret available?" must answer it the way the launch will. The desktop surface never reads secret files.
- **maestro-lib as a library for a program outside this repository:** `src/shared/maestro-lib/index.ts`, with `MAESTRO_LIB_VERSION` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/version.ts`. The entry re-exports what an outside program needs to plan, run, stream, resume and stop a turn and read its outcome, plus the parsers and the host hooks; everything else under `src/shared/maestro-lib/` is internal. `npm run build:maestro-lib` bundles it into `dist/maestro-lib/` (CommonJS, declarations, a private `package.json` carrying the version, and `docs/maestro-lib.md` as its README). The user-facing documentation is `docs/maestro-lib.md`; do not restate it here or in a README beside the code. **Who imports through it:** code outside this repository imports only the entry. Code inside the repository still reaches into deep paths and is being moved onto the entry one caller at a time, so before moving a caller onto the entry, check that every symbol it imports from a deep path is already exported here. No test enforces this; the source-scanning drift guard that used to was removed on `rc`. The entry carries the turn API this entry was built for and, since 0.3.0, the headless runtime's surface (paths and doctor, the lock primitive and data-dir lock, the in-process runtime and `MaestroClient`, store readers, Auto Run, group chat and consults). **Changing the surface:** add or remove the export in `index.ts`, bump `MAESTRO_LIB_VERSION` (patch for a fix, minor for an addition, major for a removal; below 1.0 a minor may break), and update `docs/maestro-lib.md`. `src/shared/maestro-lib/__tests__/docs-examples.test.ts` type-checks every `ts` block on that page against the entry and compares its provider table, refusal reasons and version with the library, `src/shared/maestro-lib/__tests__/built-entry.test.ts` loads the built bundle in plain Node, and `src/shared/maestro-lib/__tests__/no-desktop-framework.smoke.test.ts` walks the entry's import graph for Electron and `src/main`. A gap the terminal UI finds is fixed in the library and exported here, never worked around in the UI by reaching past the entry.
- **Escaping for a Windows cmd.exe launch:** `escapeCmdArgs()`, `windowsShellReason()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/windows-shell-escape.ts` (the escapers) and `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/windows-command.ts` (when a shell is needed). Not the POSIX `shellEscape()`. Node refuses to start an npm `.cmd` shim without a shell (`spawn EINVAL`), so `windowsShellReason()` decides when the shell is needed, `quoteCommandForCmdShell()` quotes a command path with spaces, and `escapeCmdArgs()` / `escapeArgsForShell()` escape the arguments. The desktop spawners import them through `src/main/process-manager/utils/shellEscape.ts`, which re-exports them, so their tests mock that path unchanged. For a turn spec, `applyWindowsShellRules(spec)` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/run/start-turn.ts` applies all three and is a no-op for a spec that already names its shell. The escaper doubles `^` inside quotes and cannot stop `%NAME%` expansion or a line break, so a prompt bound for a shell launch goes over stdin; `cmdShellArgProblem()` names an argument cmd.exe cannot carry.
- **Launching an agent (env layers, prompt delivery, SSH target):** `buildAgentLaunchPlan()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/launch-plan.ts`, over `buildAgentEnvironment()` / `resolveAgentEnvVars()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/env.ts`, `resolvePromptDelivery()` / `resolveSystemPromptDelivery()` / `buildPromptArgv()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/prompt-delivery.ts`, and `resolveSshLaunchTarget()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/ssh-remote-resolver.ts`. Desktop chat (`handle-spawn.ts`), Cue (`cue-spawn-builder.ts`) and the CLI (`agent-spawner.ts`) each build their own ARGUMENTS, and everything after that is decided here. **One place is not one behavior.** The three surfaces layer an agent's environment differently and deliver its prompt differently, and did so before this module existed; the caller names its `surface` (`AgentEnvSurface`: `'desktop' | 'cli' | 'cue'`) and the plan reproduces that surface exactly. Desktop: `process.env` with the Electron/IDE vars stripped `< globalShellEnvVars < defaultEnvVars < (sessionCustomEnvVars ?? agentCustomEnvVars) < readOnlyEnvOverrides < Maestro-stated vars < MAESTRO_QUERY_SOURCE`, `~/` expanded, a blank value unsets the variable, `MAESTRO_SESSION_RESUMED` stamped on a resume. CLI: `process.env` as inherited with PATH expanded; `defaultEnvVars` and `batchModeEnvVars` fill ONLY what the shell left unset (a user who exported the variable in the shell that runs the command keeps it), then the user's vars, the read-only overrides and the query source override; values are written as given; the global Settings vars are not applied. Cue: `process.env` as inherited with the desktop spawn PATH `< defaultEnvVars < user vars < read-only < MAESTRO_QUERY_SOURCE`; no global Settings vars. Making the three agree (global above the defaults, stripping Electron's vars everywhere) is a PRODUCT change with its own regressions, for example a CLI user's exported `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT` silently losing to the default, so it is proposed on its own and never folded into a refactor of this module. The session record REPLACES the provider-level one rather than layering over it on every surface, the rule `effectiveAgentCustomEnvVars()` and `resolveAgentEnvironment()` share, so usage attribution keeps describing the process that actually runs. `resolveAgentEnvVars()` is the record Maestro sets, WITHOUT `process.env` and WITHOUT the global Settings vars: what crosses to an SSH remote and what Process Details lists. Desktop's SSH wrapper (`wrap-spawn-for-ssh.ts`) still merges the global Settings vars beneath that record into the remote environment, as `rc` does; keeping them off the remote (a global path names a directory on this machine, not on the remote) would be a behavior change of its own. **An unresolvable SSH remote is an error, never a local run:** `resolveSshLaunchTarget()` names the reason (no remote selected, deleted, disabled, no remote list) in a message fit to show as-is, and the plan fails before any side effect. This is the one deliberate behavior change in the plan: desktop and Cue used to run the agent LOCALLY against the remote's path, because `getSshRemoteConfig()` folds all of those cases into "local". **Prompt delivery follows the surface too**: desktop moves the prompt to stdin on a Windows host for an agent that declared `supportsPromptViaStdin` (omp exits 0 with no output when handed stdin); the CLI and Cue keep it on the command line on every host; stream-json when images ride along; the SSH script on a remote. Desktop re-runs `resolvePromptDelivery()` on its FINAL prompt, after the system prompt and preambles are folded in. Do NOT hand-roll another `promptArgs ? ... : noPromptSeparator ? ... : ['--', prompt]` chain (there were five) or another env merge at a spawn site.
- **Starting, streaming, stopping and resuming an agent turn:** `startTurn(spec, handlers, options)` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/run/start-turn.ts`, `runTurn()` / `runToCompletion()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/run/run-to-completion.ts` over `TurnCapture` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/run/turn-capture.ts`, and `planSessionTurn()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/run/session.ts`, with the headless program in `src/shared/maestro-lib/bin/run-turn.ts`. Four pieces of code used to own a process each: desktop's `ChildProcessSpawner`, the CLI's `spawnClaudeAgent` and `spawnJsonLineAgent`, and Cue's `runProcess`, and no two shared an implementation of "start it, deliver the prompt, decode stdout on the stream, split it into lines, parse each one, stop it, report how it ended". `startTurn` is that implementation, and all four now start through it. Desktop passes the stream on unparsed (`onStdout`) to its own `StdoutHandler`, which knows four framings the run layer does not (newline-delimited JSON, Copilot's concatenated objects, batch JSON, raw text), and emits every renderer event from those callbacks in the order it always did: `raw-stdout`, then the handler; `stderr`; then `exit`. It keeps stdin OPEN for an interactive process (`keepStdinOpen`), because `ProcessManager.write()` talks to it later, and it runs the command through a shell on Windows when `windowsShellReason()` says so (`spec.shell`). One consequence of settling through `done`: a provider that fails to start is reported ONCE. Node follows ENOENT's `error` with a `close`, and the old spawner ran both handlers, so the renderer heard two `exit` events for one failed spawn. It takes a `TurnProcessSpec` (command, args, cwd, env, and text for stdin), which is the last word after planning and any SSH or wrapper step; a local `AgentLaunchPlan` becomes one through `turnProcessSpecFromPlan()`, a remote plan through the SSH wrapper first. What it guarantees: every handler for a chunk has returned before the next chunk is read, and every handler has returned before `done` resolves, so a caller that settles on `done` has seen it all; the last line is flushed even without a trailing newline; stdout is decoded on the stream, so a multibyte character split across two reads survives; `interrupt()` / `terminate()` / `terminateNow()` run the shared stop ladder and mark the turn `interrupted` BEFORE the first signal; an `AbortSignal` does the same from `terminate`; a command that does not exist is reported through `done` as `spawnError` rather than thrown, because the OS only says so after `spawn` returned. It decides nothing about SUCCESS: the caller resolves `TurnExit` with `resolveTurnOutcome` and its own options, and keeps its own timeouts. Three things are opt-in because callers disagreed: `maxLineLength` (the CLI caps a stuck line at 1 MB, Cue never did), `emptyStdin: 'ignore'` (Cue gives a child the null device, because Codex `exec` announces it is waiting on stdin before it notices a closed pipe), and how much of each stream `TurnExit` keeps (`stdoutTailLimit`, `stderrTailLimit`): the CLI keeps all of stderr because it prints it as the error, `runTurn` bounds both, and desktop and Cue pass `0` because they hold their own copy and a second one would only grow for as long as the process lives. An error on the child's stdin (EPIPE, from a prompt written to a process that already went) is handled inside `startTurn` and handed to `onStdinError`; with no listener it is an uncaught exception that takes the host down. When it is the prompt's own write that failed, `TurnExit.stdinError` keeps it and `resolveTurnOutcome` reports the turn `crashed` (rule 3b), because a clean exit after an undelivered prompt is not a finished turn; a later write by a caller that keeps stdin open is not charged to the turn. `TurnCapture` is what a turn's parsed events add up to (first session id announced, result text else streamed deltas, usage combined by `usageReportingFor(agentId)`: Codex's running total becomes deltas, Claude's terminal result wins outright, everyone else sums), and it classifies in-band failures unless told not to; the CLI's generic path passes `classifyInBandErrors: false` so its outcomes stay exactly what they were, while its Claude path keeps its own raw-message reader because Claude's `modelUsage` is richer than the parser's usage event. `runTurn` is the buffered form for a caller that only wants the finished turn. `planSessionTurn` looks the provider up, finds its binary, builds batch arguments with resume through `buildAgentArgs`, and plans the launch as the CLI does, local only. It refuses, before anything starts, what the runner could not deliver: a provider with no output parser (the turn would run and its answer could not be read) and a read-only turn for a provider whose CLI cannot enforce one (`readOnlyCliEnforced`), because a program with nobody watching must get read-only or a refusal. `maestro-lib-run` is that as a program: `npm run build` builds it, `bin` registers it, the packaged app carries it in its resources beside `maestro-p.js`, its test bundles it with esbuild and runs it under plain `node`, and it pauses the agent's stdout while its own is full, so a slow reader cannot grow it without bound. Do NOT add another `spawn()` + `stdout.on('data')` loop at an agent call site, and do NOT put a completion rule inside `startTurn`.
- **Stopping a process (interrupt, terminate, kill the tree):** `stopProcess(target, options)` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/control/termination.ts`, over `snapshotProcessTree()` / `refreshProcessTree()` / `killSurvivors()` / `killProcessTreeNow()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/control/process-tree.ts` and `killPty()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/control/pty-kill.ts`. One ladder for every caller: SIGINT (Ctrl+C on stdin or the PTY on Windows), then SIGTERM or `taskkill /t /f`, then SIGKILL for the whole tree. Desktop (`ProcessManager.interrupt()` / `kill()`), Cue (`killCueProcess()`), the CLI (`linkAbortSignal()`) and the pianola supervisor each carried a copy of the ladder, and the copies had drifted on every part of it. Desktop's interrupt decided whether to escalate by reading `child.killed`, which Node sets as soon as a signal is SENT, so on POSIX an agent that ignored SIGINT was never escalated and the tab stayed busy. Desktop's kill never followed SIGTERM with SIGKILL for a pipe child, the CLI had no Windows tree kill, and `taskkill /t /f` was spelled out in six files. None of them stopped what the agent had started: an OpenCode turn stopped mid-tool exits on the signal and leaves the tool running as an orphan, still doing its work with nothing left to stop it (measured on a real OpenCode: the turn itself closes, the `sleep` its shell tool started keeps running). The rules the module holds so no caller restates them: stop is recorded (`onStopRequested`) BEFORE the first signal, so the exit resolves as `interrupted` and not as a crash caused by our own SIGTERM; a process is running while `exitCode` and `signalCode` are both null; every stage schedules the next, for pipes and PTYs alike; descendants are recorded BEFORE the first signal (a child is re-parented the moment its parent dies and no longer leads back to it), re-read every `TREE_REFRESH_MS` while the stop is pending (an agent that is winding down can still start a tool after the first record was taken) and swept once the process exits; and a pid is only signalled when its start time still matches the recording, because seconds pass between the two and a freed pid can be handed to an unrelated process. A tree is only killed when the pid is provably a running child of this process (`ProcessTreeSnapshot.owned`); anything else gets the signal through its own handle and nothing more, which is also what keeps a test's made-up pid from ever reaching a real process. Where the record comes from depends on the platform: macOS asks `ps -o lstart`, Linux reads `/proc/<pid>/stat` directly (BusyBox `ps`, on Alpine and in many containers, cannot report `lstart`, and the tick count in `/proc` is an exact identity), and Windows records nothing because `taskkill /t` walks the tree itself. Agents are found by PARENT LINK, never by process group: real OpenCode and real Claude Code both run a tool in its OWN group, so a group kill of the agent's group returns ESRCH and reaches nothing. One ladder runs per process: a second `stopProcess` on the same target (Stop pressed twice, a tab closed while a Stop is pending) advances the ladder already running instead of stacking a second set of timers. `from` and `graceMs` are required so each caller STATES its choice: a user Stop starts at `interrupt`, a closed tab, a timeout or an aborted run at `terminate`, and the grace is `INTERACTIVE_STOP_GRACE_MS` (2 s) for a turn someone is watching or `BACKGROUND_STOP_GRACE_MS` (5 s) for an unattended run. `immediate` is the shutdown path (every remaining stage at once, no timer, no exit listener, per MAESTRO-3B), `blocking` waits for `taskkill` so the tree is gone before the host exits, and `upTo` caps the ladder at a stage. **Quitting the desktop app sends what it always has**, through `upTo`: a pipe-backed agent gets SIGTERM and nothing behind it, so it can finish writing its transcript or session file (a SIGKILL sent right after would take that away, and at quit there is no event loop left to wait out a grace period), a PTY gets SIGKILL, Windows gets a blocking `taskkill`, and descendants are left alone. When `taskkill` fails for a child that is still running, the child is ended through its own handle, so a stop never leaves it running. Two things are deliberately NOT routed through the ladder. A PTY interrupt stays a bare Ctrl+C with no escalation, because a PTY process that survives Ctrl+C is the normal case (a shell that cancelled a command, a TUI that cancelled its turn and is waiting for input). And a terminal tab passes `includeDescendants: false`, because a job the user left running in their own shell is theirs to keep. `taskkill` is called directly, never through a shell. Do NOT hand-roll another `kill('SIGTERM')` + `setTimeout` pair at a spawn site, and do NOT read `child.killed` to decide anything.
- **Whether a Claude turn runs the maestro-p TUI or `claude --print`:** `resolveClaudeSpawnModeCore()` / `applyClaudeSpawnDecision()` / `buildRemoteInteractiveSpawn()` / `isMaestroPBinaryPath()` in `src/shared/maestro-lib/launch/interactive-mode.ts`, over `selectMode()` in `launch/claude-mode-selector.ts`. One decision function for chat, Auto Run, tab naming, synopsis, group chat and Cue, with the native-backed deps supplied by `src/main/agents/resolveClaudeSpawnMode.ts` and lightweight ones by the CLI. `src/main/agents/claudeSpawnCore.ts` and `claude-mode-selector.ts` are re-exports of it.
